Key parity unchanged at 1080. (`Strings.uk.resx`; RemEx-lnf3.)
- **Ukrainian and Spanish couldn't tell an upload from a download.** Three transfer messages were byte-identical to their opposite twin, so "Завантаження завершено." appeared whether a file had gone up or come down — and the wrong side differed by language. Ukrainian had the *upload* strings using the download word (`Завантаження` where the app's own button says `Вивантажити ↑`); Spanish had the *download* completion using the upload word (`Subida completada.` where its button says `Descargar ↓`). Ukrainian also used a third word, `Відправлення`, for upload in one place, so it now says `Вивантаження` in all seven direction-labelling upload strings rather than six of seven with an outlier. The direction of each fix was confirmed against the call sites, not just the vocabulary — `FileTransferViewModel` puts each key on the branch its name claims, so the strings were wrong rather than the wiring. The same defect turned out to be worse on the PC side — see the Ukrainian entry above, where it reached fourteen strings. (`values-es/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-0lyw.)
- **Polish on Android called the card-opacity slider "transparency" too.** `Przezroczystość karty: 100%` on a fully solid card — the slider raises opacity, so the label described the opposite. It now reads `Krycie karty`, the term Polish Photoshop, GIMP and Affinity all use, matching what the PC side settled on so the two platforms agree. The FAQ bullet listing what Personalization can change carried the same word and is corrected with it; leaving it would have had the answer contradict the control it describes. Polish was the last locale — Ukrainian and Turkish were fixed earlier — and the sweep confirming that was validated against the pre-fix file first, because a scan that reports "clean" proves nothing until you have shown it can report dirty. (`values-pl/strings.xml`; RemEx-svuo.)
- **Removed a leaked shell variable from all nine Android string files.** A bare `$line` sat as text directly inside `<resources>` in every locale, at the same position in each — an unexpanded variable left behind by a script that appended strings in 2.2.0. It never broke anything because aapt2 ignores text nodes inside `<resources>`, which is exactly why it survived four releases: nothing in the build could see it. Verified lossless rather than assumed: the key sets are byte-identical before and after in all nine files, every one of the 768 English keys is present in all eight locales, and all 656 `R.string` references across the module resolve — with no dynamic name lookups anywhere, so the compiler is a complete oracle. (9 `strings.xml` files; RemEx-nmuu.)
- **The Background Mode and Scheme Variant pickers no longer show developer identifiers.** They listed raw internal names — `Mica`, `TonalSpot`, `FruitSalad`, `Spritz` — identically in all nine languages. Thirteen labels are now localized (key count 1067 → 1080), while `SelectedItem` keeps binding the raw identifier, which is load-bearing: those strings are written to and compared from the saved dashboard profile, so localizing the bound value would silently reset every user's backdrop and colour scheme. Not everything was translated — `Mica` and `Acrylic` are Windows backdrop API names Microsoft itself leaves in English and sit adjacent in the same dropdown, `Spritz` has no sense to render, and `Tonal Spot` stays English because every literal translation was wrong (the Ukrainian one meant "tonal *stain*", on the default variant). `Content` became "From Content" and its equivalents, since the bare word explains nothing and the French adjective *content* means "pleased". The labels also refresh when the language is switched live, which the first attempt at this got wrong: a ComboBox item is a plain string and raises no change notification, so the labels would have frozen in the previous language while the panel around them — including the language picker a dozen lines above — translated correctly. A new `LocalizationService.CultureTag` gives bindings something notifying to observe that no future translation pass can rename away. Reviewed by Opus twice, the first attempt reverted outright on that regression. (`PersonalizationPanelView.axaml`, new `PrefixedLabelConverter.cs`, `LocalizationService.cs`, 9 resx files; RemEx-suc9.)
- **Polish, Ukrainian, Turkish and Indonesian labelled the opacity sliders "transparency" — the opposite of what they do.** Drag the card-opacity slider up and the card becomes *more solid*; those four languages told users it would become more see-through. The slider is genuinely opacity — `GlassOpacity` feeds an alpha channel, so 1.0 is fully opaque — and the labels now say so: Polish `Krycie` (the term Polish Photoshop, GIMP and Affinity all use), Ukrainian `Непрозорість`, Turkish `Opaklık`, Indonesian `opasitas`. Sixteen values in all, because the row tooltip, the slider tooltip and the customization glossary carried the same inversion as the label and would otherwise have contradicted it on the same hover target. Turkish `Matlık` was rejected for the same reason a Ukrainian string saying `матовості` was corrected: *matteness* is a surface finish, not alpha — a different wrong meaning rather than a fix. This is the PC twin of a defect also present on Android, where a Ukrainian opacity slider was labelled `Прозорість` (Ukrainian and Turkish were corrected earlier; Polish followed in RemEx-svuo below); the same mistake arising independently on both platforms points at the English "Glass frosting opacity percentage" on a Clear→Frosted axis, which reasonably reads as a frosting control (tracked in RemEx-b95g). Reviewed by Opus across three rounds — it caught that fixing only the labels left them contradicting their own tooltips, and then that the verification sweep itself was blind to inflected forms. (`Strings.pl.resx`, `Strings.uk.resx`, `Strings.tr.resx`, `Strings.id.resx`; RemEx-rawm.)
- **The sensor colour-preset menu now translates.** Its eight entries — Default, Magenta & Cyan, Smoke & Gold, Emerald Night, Sunset, Arctic, Neon Purple, Monochrome — were hardcoded English headers, so they stayed English in all nine languages. Note this contradicts an earlier entry below, which claimed the preset menu was "localized across all 9 desktop cultures"; the *card* menu was, these eight headers were not. Only the displayed header changed: each `CommandParameter` keeps its English literal, because `ApplyTheme` matches the preset by exact name and then silently does nothing when it finds none — so translating the parameter would have disabled every preset with no error anywhere. Key count 1059 → 1067. Reviewed by Opus (PASS), which diffed the file against `HEAD` to confirm all 21 command parameters and 23 command bindings are byte-identical and that the only changed attributes are the eight headers, then checked each parameter still matches a real entry in `SensorCardTheme.Presets`. It also caught that Turkish `Kutup` means *polar* rather than Arctic, where every other locale uses the cognate, and that the Turkish values were sentence case while that file's other menu items are Title Case — both corrected. (`CanvasView.axaml`, 9 resx files; RemEx-suc9.2.)
- **Six labels in Task Manager and Remote Desktop now translate.** `PID:`, `Path:`, `Publisher:`, `Resolution:`, `FPS:` and `Q:` were hardcoded English in XAML `StringFormat`, so they stayed English in all nine languages. Three of the six resource keys turned out to **already exist, already translated in every locale, and be referenced by nothing** — the English was hardcoded three lines away in the same file. All six now go through the existing `LocalizedFormatConverter`, which is precisely what that converter was written for: its own documentation notes that nesting a localization binding inside `StringFormat` throws at view construction, which is why the terse hardcoded form had persisted. `Q:` also expands to the full word for Quality — an unexplained one-letter abbreviation is exactly what this project's plain-language standard exists to prevent — and keeps its `%`, so it agrees with the quality control shown above it rather than reading as a unit-less restatement. Turkish places the percent sign before the number and French keeps its space, per each locale's own convention. Key count 1056 → 1059. Reviewed by Opus (PASS), which confirmed the three keys pre-dated the change and were referenced nowhere, and checked that every attribute survived the rewrite from a self-closing element to a `MultiBinding` — including the one status field that uses a different brush from its neighbours. (`TaskManagerView.axaml`, `RemoteDesktopView.axaml`, 9 resx files; RemEx-suc9.1.)
- **Ctrl+K → "Force Shutdown" no longer shuts the PC down without asking.** The command palette wired its power entries straight to the underlying connection commands, bypassing the confirmations the Remote page applies — so the same action prompted from one surface and executed instantly from the other. Shutdown, Force Shutdown, Restart and Restart to Firmware now confirm, reusing the *existing* confirmation text the Remote page already uses, so the two surfaces say the same thing and no new strings were needed. Lock, Sleep, Hibernate and Wake-on-LAN stay unprompted, matching the Remote page's policy for recoverable actions. The palette closes before the prompt appears, which is the only workable order: the palette is itself modal over the main window and dismisses itself when it loses focus, so keeping it open would both break the modality chain and race its own close against the dialog. Fail-closed like the others — no owner window means the entry declines rather than runs — and the entry type now refuses at construction to be given only some of its confirmation keys, since a half-marked entry would otherwise fail *open* and run unconfirmed with nothing reporting it. Reviewed by Opus (PASS), which verified the generated command still binds from the Enter-key handler and that closing the palette cannot lose the entry or the delegate mid-flight. (`CommandPaletteEntry.cs`, `CommandPaletteViewModel.cs`, `ShellViewModel.cs`; RemEx-eifi.)
- **Six destructive PC actions now ask before they act.** Ending a process, removing a launcher card, removing a shared folder, revoking a phone's file access, restoring default shared folders and clearing the diagnostic log all applied instantly on a single click, with no undo. Each now shows the same confirmation dialog the power actions and remote file delete already used, naming what is about to happen — the process and its PID, the folder, the phone — and saying plainly what survives: removing a launcher card does not uninstall the program, and unsharing a folder does not delete its files. Revoking trust is the sharpest of the six, since one mis-click cost a phone its file access until the user paired and approved it again. The guards **fail closed**: a view that has not supplied a dialog, or cannot host one because the window is hidden to the tray, cancels the action rather than performing it unconfirmed. For the two Settings actions the confirmation lives in the ViewModel's event handler rather than the list item's command, because that is where the deletion actually happens. The dialog lambda that two views had duplicated now lives in one `ConfirmationDialogHost`, so the six new call sites did not become six more copies. Seven new localized strings across all nine languages (key count 1043 → 1056), following the existing `"<Action> — Are you sure?"` heading convention rather than inventing a second one. Reviewed by Opus, which traced every guarded method to all of its entry points to confirm no path reaches the destructive work ahead of the guard. (`TaskManagerViewModel.cs`, `AppLauncherViewModel.cs`, `SettingsViewModel.cs`, `DiagnosticLogsViewModel.cs`, 5 views, new `ConfirmationDialogHost.cs`, 9 resx files; RemEx-6p1f.)
- **Screen readers in Turkish and Ukrainian now describe the controls, and two Turkish keycaps that broke voice control are fixed.** Eighteen icon-only controls — refresh, back, collapse/expand, stop, dismiss, scroll, zoom, reset, customise cards, fullscreen, the remote-desktop frame — announced their names in English to tr and uk users. Now translated. Separately, two Turkish keycap descriptions were live **WCAG 2.5.3 Label in Name** failures: `cd_key_tab` said `Sekme` and `cd_key_delete` said `Sil`, while the on-screen chips draw the hardcoded Latin `Tab` and `Del`. Because a `contentDescription` on a merged Compose node replaces its descendant text outright, the accessible name contained none of the visible label, so a Turkish user with voice control saying the "Tab" they could see had nothing to match. Both restored to the legend, which is also what Turkish keyboards print. The remaining keycaps were deliberately left alone: `Alt`, `AltGr`, `Shift`, `Home`, `End`, `Esc` and `Ins` already contain their legend, and the glyph-only keys (`⌫`, `↵`, `⊞`, arrows) draw no text for 2.5.3 to constrain. `PgUp`/`PgDn` are left to RemEx-qykh because they fail in **all nine** locales including English, and picking a form for two locales would strand that fix — as would English's own `cd_key_ctrl`, which says "Control" against a drawn `Ctrl` while tr and uk already say `Ctrl`. Key parity unchanged at 768 strings per locale. Reviewed by Opus (PASS), which traced all 21 touched keys to their call sites and established that two of them are visible FAB-menu labels rather than content descriptions despite the `cd_` prefix — translating them was required, not merely safe, and the misleading prefix is filed as RemEx-ynz7. (`values-tr/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.8.)
- **Turkish and Ukrainian now translate the screens, tabs, banners and error messages that were still English.** Screen titles, the settings tabs, the offline banner and snackbars, the local-network permission error, the Task Manager and File Transfer labels and the splash tagline all showed English to tr and uk users. All are now translated, grounded in each file's own vocabulary rather than a dictionary — which twice meant changing *my* wording rather than the existing prose: `nav_more_label` became `Diğer` because the Turkish FAQ had called that tab `Diğer` all along, and the dashboard title became `RemEx Ana Paneli` / `Головна панель RemEx` to match the `Ana panel` / `головна панель` each file already used. Translating a screen title invalidates any help text that names it, and the help text deliberately named these screens *in English* because English is what the UI showed — so nine prose values were corrected in the same commit, found by sweeping every key for English UI names rather than trusting a list of expected citers, which beat the expected list three separate times. The dashboard prose went further and dropped the branded title entirely, matching English and all four reference locales, which say "the dashboard" generically — that dissolves the coupling instead of re-pointing it at the next rename. Also fixed: `settings_faq_hint`, which was English in both locales *and* stale in English, so no identical-to-English check could see it, and which still described an overflow menu that is now the More tab. The `cd_*` accessibility descriptions were deliberately **not** included and moved to RemEx-c7hu.8: `RemoteDesktopScreen.kt` draws the key row with hardcoded Latin legends, so WCAG 2.5.3 Label in Name means translating those names would break voice control — a speech user saying the visible "Shift" could not match a Turkish-named control. Key parity unchanged at 768. Reviewed by Opus (PASS after one fix round; it found a fully-English key my drift-blind detector missed, rejected one do-not-translate verdict as a laundered nine-locale gap, and supplied the de-branding insight). (`values-tr/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.6.)
- **Six languages no longer tell users to enter an "access key" that was removed in 2.0.** Step 4 of the connection help instructed the user to enter a shared access key if they had set one on the PC — but `ValidateAccessKey` was deleted in 2.0, when certificate pinning plus PIN pairing replaced it. The string therefore promised authentication by a control that no longer runs, and sent the reader looking for a PC-side field that no longer exists; being told your connection is secured by a mechanism that isn't there is worse than being told nothing. English, Turkish and Ukrainian already said "enter the 6-digit PIN shown by the PC"; es, fr, hi, in, pl and pt-BR now say the same, each in its own established pairing vocabulary (`emparejamiento`, `couplage`, `पेयरिंग`, `penyandingan`, `parowanie`, `pareamento`) taken from that locale's own `faq_a5` and `pairing_title`. Only the second sentence changed — every locale's "The default port is 5005" was already correct and is byte-identical — and fr, hi and pl additionally lose the retired "host" wording in this string. The access key now appears in no Android string in any language, and no Kotlin source references it. Key parity unchanged at 768. Reviewed by Opus (PASS), which verified each conditional agrees with its own subject (including Polish neuter `sparowane` and the feminine agreement Hindi `पेयरिंग` takes), and confirmed the PIN field is on the same screen so the new instruction is actionable where it appears. (6 locale `strings.xml` files; RemEx-8p8g.)
- **Security: the remaining six languages no longer tell users to expose their PC to the internet.** The English answer to "Can I connect over the internet?" was rewritten some time ago to recommend Tailscale and to say plainly that forwarding a port "exposes your PC to the internet and is a real security risk" — but every non-English locale still carried the *previous* answer, which instructed the user to do exactly that: set up port forwarding on the router for port 5005 and connect via their public IP. So the eight translated FAQs were recommending the one thing the English FAQ had been changed to warn against. Turkish and Ukrainian were corrected in RemEx-c7hu.5; `faq_a6` in es, fr, hi, in, pl and pt-BR now carries the current answer, with each locale's terminology taken from its own already-translated Tailscale walkthrough (`faq_a11`) so the two answers agree, and each forward reference quoting that locale's own question text byte-exact rather than a paraphrase the reader could not find. Two things were deliberately *not* copied from English: its "forward a port on your router to your public IP" describes an operation that does not exist (forwarding maps an external port to a private host, not to the public IP), so these six say "open a port on your router and connect via your public IP address" instead; and pt-BR's extra sentence telling users to "sempre ative a Chave de Acesso para autenticação" was dropped, because `ValidateAccessKey` was removed in 2.0 in favour of certificate pinning — that sentence was promising authentication by a control that no longer runs. Key parity unchanged at 768. Reviewed by Opus (PASS), which independently confirmed the access-key removal against `RemexNetworkListener.cs` and verified the security polarity is intact in all six. (6 locale `strings.xml` files; RemEx-jc7f.)
- **The Turkish and Ukrainian help content is translated, and three answers that had gone stale are corrected.** The FAQ, tutorial and About screens showed English to tr and uk users — 31 and 21 values respectively, including the whole tutorial and every FAQ question. All are now translated, with every term grounded in each file's own existing vocabulary rather than a dictionary (tr reuses its established `bağlantı noktası`, `Uzak Masaüstü`, `öğretici`, `eşleştirme`; uk settles on `сполучення`, which held 18 of 25 pairing occurrences and traces to `pairing_title`, the oldest of them). Three of the defects were *stale* rather than merely untranslated, which is why they survived earlier sweeps — a value holding superseded English is neither equal to current English nor in the target language, so neither kind of scan sees it. The worst was `faq_a6`: it still told tr and uk users to forward a port to their public IP, advice the English answer was rewritten specifically to replace, because it now calls that "a real security risk" and recommends Tailscale instead. `about_github_cat` was still the developer's placeholder joke, and `about_github_star` still asked for a GitHub star after the English changed to "Follow". Also fixed: `tutorial_page6_body`, which had drifted from English in both locales; the ipconfig line label in `faq_a2` and `tutorial_page4_body`, now `IPv4 Adresi` / `IPv4-адреса` as localized Windows actually prints it; and uk `faq_a7`, which cited "Remote Desktop Defaults" after that label had been translated. Prose names each screen exactly as the UI displays it today, which for `RemEx Home Base`, `Connection`, `Personalization` and `FAQ` still means English — pointing a user at a name their screen does not show is the defect this replaces, not a compromise. Key parity unchanged at 768 per locale; every new apostrophe and quote escaped, verified against the compiled APK with `aapt2 dump resources` rather than the source. Reviewed by Opus (PASS after two fix rounds; it caught the third stale key, two further inflections of the retired Ukrainian term, and an adjective that made one answer contradict itself). (`values-tr/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.5.)
- **Pairing failures are now translated instead of showing English developer diagnostics.** Every failure path in the pairing exports returned a string built for a log — `"ERROR: TLS/upgrade timed out after 20s — TCP reached 192.168.1.5:5005 but TLS handshake or WebSocket upgrade did not complete (check host cert and that path '/ws' is mapped)"` — and both the pairing screen and the auto-pair path printed it to the user verbatim, in English, in all 9 languages, complete with the address, port and certificate internals. `Remex.Core` is shared and NativeAOT and has no access to Android resources, so it cannot translate anything itself; the fix is for it to say *which* failure happened and let the app pick the sentence. All 21 returns now carry a stable machine cause code (`PairingErrorCodes`, e.g. `TLS_TIMEOUT`, `PIN_REJECTED`) ahead of the diagnostic, `PairingErrors.parse`/`messageRes` map the code to an existing translated string, and the diagnostic goes to logcat — which is app-private, where the screen it used to appear on was shoulder-surfable. Not a protocol change: these strings cross the JNI boundary in-process and `libRemexCore.so` ships in the same APK, so no host or `protocolVersion` coordination is involved, and pairing behaviour is untouched. A codeless failure from an older library degrades to a single generic localized message rather than breaking. The advice is also now chosen per **surface**, because the same cause needs opposite instructions in the two places pairing can fail: the dedicated pairing screen keeps Submit enabled while re-using a session that is already dead, so there the only escape is **Cancel**, whereas the connection screen has no Cancel button and restarts pairing on every tap, so there the fix really is to enter the PC's current PIN and try again. Telling someone to tap a button their screen does not have would have been worse than the English it replaced. `pairing_error_bad_pin` — dead code until now — supplies the connection-screen wording, extended in all 9 locales with the missing next step; key parity unchanged at 768. 14 tests cover the parser and both surfaces, including one that fails if any mapped cause loses its explanation and one that fails if a surface quietly gets worse advice than the other. (`AndroidNativeExports.cs`, new `PairingErrorCodes.cs`, new `PairingErrors.kt`, `PairingScreen.kt`, `RemexClientManager.kt`, 9 `strings.xml`; RemEx-6gkr.)
- **The elevation FAQ said RemEx "will not finish starting up"; it actually opens and just cannot accept connections.** `Program.cs` catches a failed embedded-host start, nulls the host and port, and carries on — so the window appears normally and only the listener is missing. A PC user seeing RemEx running would reasonably conclude the entry did not apply to them, which defeats its purpose. All 18 values corrected (PC `Faq_Q13_Answer` × 9, Android `faq_a14` × 9); the following clause, that the phone cannot reach it even though it still shows as paired, was already the true symptom and is unchanged. One clause replaced per value, leaving the rest byte-identical, and every replacement written apostrophe-free so the Android escaping hazard cannot apply. Parity 1043 PC / 767 Android. (9 resx files, 9 `strings.xml`; RemEx-usqu.)
- **The English FAQ named a manifest constant instead of the toggle Android shows.** `Faq_Q4_Answer` step 3 said to "allow Nearby Wi-Fi Devices" — that is `NEARBY_WIFI_DEVICES`, the constant, not the "Nearby devices" label in Android Settings, so an English-speaking user searching for it would not find it. This was the only occurrence of the constant name in any shipped resource: RemEx-9fn5 had already translated that step into all eight locales using each one's real Settings label, which left the neutral English as the least findable of the nine. The Android FAQ's equivalent answer does not name the permission at all, so nothing needed aligning there. Key parity 1043. (`Strings.resx`; RemEx-jbot.)
- **The About page claimed all traffic is TLS 1.3 while the host also accepts TLS 1.2.** `HostBootstrapper.cs:240` sets `SslProtocols.Tls13 | SslProtocols.Tls12`, so "All traffic is now encrypted with TLS 1.3" was not guaranteed by configuration — true in practice for the shipping Android client, which negotiates 1.3, but the sentence describes the transport, and a security claim on the About page is one a reader is entitled to take literally. All nine locales now say traffic is encrypted with TLS 1.3 where the client supports it and never below TLS 1.2. `SslProtocols` is deliberately untouched: dropping 1.2 is a compatibility decision about older paired clients, not a copy edit. Only the first sentence changed per file. Key parity 1043. (9 resx files; RemEx-kg71.)
- **A non-interactive start was labelled "Service PC", and the About page printed the raw wire token.** `Status_ServiceHost` implied RemEx can be installed as a service; it cannot. The branch is reachable though — `HostCapabilitiesProvider` returns `"service"` when `Environment.UserInteractive && SessionId != 0` is false on Windows, the same condition Linux reports as `"headless"` — so this is a detected state, not a shipped mode, and the label was corrected rather than the branch deleted. All nine locales now say the host has no desktop session, keeping each file's existing noun. A second, worse instance surfaced while verifying: `AboutViewModel` interpolated the raw `RuntimeMode` token into the About page, so a non-interactive start displayed the literal untranslated word "service" in every language. The localizing switch already existed in `ConnectionViewModel` but About bypassed it; it is now extracted as `HostRuntimeLabel` and shared, so the mapping exists once instead of once-and-a-bypass. (9 resx files, `ConnectionViewModel.cs`, `AboutViewModel.cs`, `HostCapabilitiesProvider.cs`; RemEx-9z0f.)
- **The Android Settings screen was titled in English for Ukrainian and Turkish users.** `screen_settings_title`, `cd_settings` and `cd_settings_fab` were all the raw literal "Settings" in `values-uk` and `values-tr` — and two of the three are accessibility labels, so TalkBack announced them in English too. Both files already used the correct term elsewhere (`cd_close_settings` = "Закрити налаштування" / "Ayarları kapat"), so the fix takes each file's own word: `Налаштування` and `Ayarlar`. Key parity 767. (2 `strings.xml`; RemEx-z98p.)
- **The Android Unpair button was English in all eight languages.** `connection_unpair` was the raw literal "Unpair" in every locale file. That undercut other work rather than just looking untidy: several pairing-failure and certificate-change messages tell the user to unpair the PC and pair again, and that instruction is unfollowable when the button it names is labelled in a language the user did not pick. Each translation is derived from the term its own file already uses, with the sibling button `connection_action_repair` as the register and length model — `Desemparejar`, `Découpler`, `अनपेयर करें`, `Lepaskan`, `Usuń sparowanie`, `Desparear`, `Eşleştirmeyi kaldır`, `Скасувати спарювання`. French takes `Découpler` rather than a form of *appairage* because that file establishes coupler/couplage (10 occurrences against 4), including in `pairing_error_generic` and `faq_q5`. Key parity 767. (8 `strings.xml`; RemEx-wjbc.)
- **Eighteen Hindi strings ended with the wrong script's full stop.** `Strings.hi.resx` terminated 18 values with the CJK ideographic full stop `U+3002` instead of the Devanagari danda `U+0964`. Two earlier beads each tripped over a single instance; this was a systematic artifact of however those translations were produced, so all 18 were swept at once — the file now has zero. The Android Hindi file was checked and is clean, so this was PC-only. Two confirmation titles were also brought back into line with the family established by RemEx-v5it: Hindi's delete title moves from `सुनिश्चित` (closer to "ensured") to `निश्चित`, the accurate "sure" the five power titles use; Ukrainian's moves from the infinitive `Видалити` to the verbal noun `Видалення`, matching its own `Вимкнення`/`Перезавантаження` and making the file internally consistent as Polish already was. Key parity 1044. (`Strings.hi.resx`, `Strings.uk.resx`; RemEx-zmmk.)
- **The Android FAQ now names the same 5009 port bound as the PC one.** It said the fallback goes to "the next free port (5006, 5007, ...)" with no upper limit, which made it the vaguer of the two once the PC side was corrected. Only the parenthetical was replaced in each of the nine files — deliberately surgical, because several of these values carry escaped apostrophes and rewriting whole values is what forced two reverts elsewhere. French uses "au plus 5009" and Turkish "5009 dahil" specifically so the inserted text needs no apostrophe escape. Android keeps the detail the PC text omits: `remex.agent` first tries to reclaim the port from a stale instance of itself, so the common case stays on 5005. (9 `strings.xml`; RemEx-xs83.)
- **The PC FAQ understated the port fallback as a single alternative.** `Faq_Q5_Answer` said port 5005 "will fall back to 5006", but `HostBootstrapper.cs:193-195` loops five attempts at `port + attempt`, so the real range is 5005 through 5009. All nine locales now say RemEx tries the next free port — 5006, 5007 and so on, up to 5009 — one changed clause per file, surrounding sentences untouched. The Android FAQ already had this right and additionally notes that remex.agent first tries to reclaim the port from a stale instance of itself, which is real (`StalePortReclaimer.TryReclaim`, guarded to the canonical port), so the common case stays on 5005; Android is now the vaguer of the two for lacking an upper bound and is tracked separately. (9 resx files; RemEx-j5ru.)
- **Failed canvas snapshots were reported in success green.** `SetSnapshotStatus` is the single funnel for that status line, and every message went through it — "Saved to …" and "Copied to clipboard" alongside "Export failed: …", "Copy failed: …" and "Clipboard unavailable" — while the `TextBlock` had one fixed `SystemSuccessBrush`. So a failed export announced itself in the colour reserved for success. The funnel now carries the outcome (`SetSnapshotStatus(message, succeeded: false)` at the three failure sites) and the line is coloured by a class, `SystemSuccessBrush` by default and `SystemErrorBrush` when the last attempt failed. The view model's doc comment claimed it was "called after a successful snapshot export", which its own call sites had always contradicted; it now describes both paths. (`CanvasDashboardViewModel.cs`, `CanvasView.axaml`, `CanvasView.axaml.cs`; RemEx-40x8.)
- **All eight connection status indicators now animate the same way instead of three of them.** Three carried a local `BrushTransition Property="Fill"` and five had none — an inconsistency that was invisible until RemEx-udds, because `Fill` previously held one brush instance whose `Color` was mutated in place, so the transition had nothing to animate and never fired. With `Fill` now swapping brush objects the three came to life and the mismatch became visible. The transition moved into the two shared `Ellipse.status-dot` / `.status-ring` styles (the ring animating `Stroke`), and the three local `Ellipse.Transitions` blocks were deleted — so the colour and its animation are defined in one place, and all eight crossfade over 0.4s on connect, disconnect and theme switch. Net five lines fewer. (`App.axaml`, 3 views; RemEx-adgq.)
- **The connection status dots ignored the theme too, and they were the hardest case.** Six indicators — the sidebar rail, tray flyout, canvas header, Home radar (ring plus two dots), Connection page and Settings — got their green/red from a literal `ConverterParameter=#22C55E|#F43F5E` (or `#4ADE80|#FF6B6B`; the app used two different pairs) passed to `BoolToColorConverter`. A converter parameter is a string, so `DynamicResource` cannot reach it, which is why RemEx-s7n0 could not fix these alongside the other literals. Teaching the converter to resolve resource keys was rejected: a converter evaluates once per binding, so switching theme at runtime would have left the dots painted in the old theme's colours. They now use the conditional-class idiom this codebase already uses for `Classes.nav-item-active` and `Classes.selected` — `Classes.connected="{Binding …IsConnected}"` against four shared `Ellipse.status-dot` / `.status-ring` styles in `App.axaml` whose setters use `SystemSuccessBrush`/`SystemErrorBrush`. That is correct on both axes: the class flips with the connection state and the brush re-resolves on a theme switch. It also collapses the two divergent colour pairs onto one semantic pair. A side effect worth knowing about: three of these ellipses carry a `BrushTransition Property="Fill"`, which never actually fired before — `Fill` was assigned one brush instance and only that instance's `Color` was mutated, so the transition had nothing to animate. Now that `Fill` genuinely swaps brush objects, the 0.4s crossfade runs for the first time, on connect/disconnect and on theme switch. `BoolToColorConverter` is left in place but now has no callers. (`App.axaml`, 6 views; RemEx-udds.)
- **Danger, warning and status colours were hardcoded, so they ignored the theme the user picked — and one security warning had no colour at all.** 18 hardcoded literals across 7 PC views now resolve through theme tokens, plus one unresolvable resource key in an 8th: `#FF4444`/`#FF6B6B`/`#F43F5E` → `SystemErrorBrush`, `#3A1A1A`/`#4A2A2A` (danger button background and hover) → `SystemErrorBackgroundBrush`/`...HoverBrush`, `#FFD93D` → `SystemWarningBrush`, `#4ADE80` status text → `SystemSuccessBrush`, and the command palette's `#22FFFFFF` pointerover → `CardBackgroundHoverBrush`. SolarFlare shows why this mattered rather than being tidiness: its `SystemError` is `#DC2626` and `SystemSuccess` `#16A34A`, tuned for a light surface, so bright dark-theme literals were being painted onto a pale background — the warning yellow in particular was close to unreadable. One new token, `SystemWarningBackgroundBrush`, was added to all four themes to give the warn tint a home, mirroring the existing `SystemErrorBackgroundBrush` (`SystemWarning` at 0.15 opacity). That is deliberately a normalisation rather than a like-for-like swap: `#15FFD93D` is 8.2% alpha, not 15%, so the warn button's tint is now ~1.8× stronger and matches the danger button it sits beside. Every token used is verified defined in **all four** themes, which is the correctness standard available without a visual pass. Separately, a sweep for unresolvable resources found `SettingsView.axaml` colouring `Settings_KeepSessionUnlockedWarning` — the security warning for letting a paired phone control a locked PC — with `{DynamicResource WarningBrush}`, a key defined in no theme and nowhere else in the app. An unresolved `DynamicResource` yields `UnsetValue` silently (unlike `StaticResource`, which throws), so `Foreground` fell through to the inherited body-text colour: the security warning was rendering **visually indistinguishable from the description line above it**, which is worse than being miscoloured. It now uses `SystemWarningBrush`. The six status indicators whose colours sit inside a literal `BoolToColorConverter` parameter cannot use `DynamicResource` without a converter or markup redesign and are tracked as RemEx-udds. (4 theme files, 8 views; RemEx-s7n0.)
- **Non-English users opening About were shown internal engineering notes, and English advertised a systemd service that no longer ships.** Two defects in the same `What's New?` block. The English `About_WhatsNew_Issues_Body` claimed "systemd user service" as a Linux feature — true when written (`installer/linux/remex-host.service` existed with `WantedBy=default.target`, so genuinely a user service) but deleted in `65ff3c8` (RemEx-u0oc) once RemEx-aep had settled the no-service architecture, leaving an XDG autostart entry in its place — so the claim had drifted into a false one. It now reads "autostart at sign-in". The worse half only appeared once the block was audited per locale: English had been restructured into five narrative feature cards while **all eight** locale files still carried the previous generation's values — bare category labels plus terse internal lists. So a Spanish, French, Hindi, Indonesian, Polish, Portuguese, Turkish or Ukrainian user saw a card headed "Fixes" whose body read *"Settings freeze (Linux), Async-void crashes, Alert memory leaks, Duplicate style blocks"*, and one headed "Issues (Known)" reading *"Bitmap-on-clipboard deferred to 2.x"* — raw bug-tracker shorthand and a deferral note, on a page shown every release. All 54 values are realigned to the current cards, with technical terms kept verbatim (NVENC, QSV, AMF, VAAPI, MediaCodec, MJPEG, TLS 1.3, ECDH P-256, SHA-256, PipeWire, Wayland, ANSI) and verified present in every locale. The block's remaining two Linux claims were checked and kept because they are true: PipeWire capture is real, and "full Wayland input injection" is delivered through the `portal-notify` path — the libei backend is a latent, unselected stub tracked as RemEx-whxz, not the active route. `About_WhatsNew` itself, still raw English in five locales, is translated too. Key parity 1042. (9 resx files; RemEx-892l.)
- **The PC tutorial promised always-on access the app cannot provide, and offered a Windows service that does not exist.** `Tutorial_P1_Body2` read "For always-on access (even when you're not logged in), you can install the PC as a Windows service or Linux PC" — wrong twice over. There is no RemEx service (`scripts/autostart-remex.ps1` registers a Task Scheduler logon task with `-AtLogOn -LogonType Interactive`; Linux uses an XDG autostart entry), and because the agent runs inside the signed-in user's session, access is inherently session-scoped — it is *not* available while nobody is logged in. All eight locales had faithfully translated that false promise. The text now says what actually happens: RemEx starts automatically at sign-in, the phone can connect whenever you are signed in, sensor monitoring and power commands survive a screen lock while streaming or controlling the desktop needs the session unlocked (remote desktop reports itself unavailable on the Winlogon secure desktop, and the "keep session unlocked" gate is opt-in and off by default), and if the PC is off or signed out you wake it with Wake-on-LAN and sign in first. `Tutorial_P0_Body` likewise dropped its hardcoded "RemEx 2.0" version string and its offer to stream to "a second desktop client" — Android is the only client. The diagnostics tab that claimed to show "Background Service Logs" actually runs `Get-EventLog -LogName Application -Source 'Remex.Agent'`, so it and its button are now the System Event Logs family, with the matching in-view-model status strings and two stale code comments corrected to stop asserting a host service that was removed. Key parity 1042 across all 9 files. (9 resx files, `DiagnosticLogsViewModel.cs`, `DiagnosticLogsView.axaml`; RemEx-u4kw.)
- **Four languages were being asked to approve Force shutdown and Restart-to-UEFI in English.** Every `Confirm_*` power-command dialog — Shut down, Restart, Force shutdown, Force restart, Reboot to UEFI — was raw English in `hi`, `pt-BR`, `tr` and `uk`, title and message both. This is the app's destructive-action surface: the dialog exists precisely so the user can decline, and the sentence that makes declining an informed choice ("All unsaved work will be lost") was one a Hindi, Brazilian Portuguese, Turkish or Ukrainian user could not necessarily read. An unreadable confirmation is worse than no confirmation, because it still looks like consent was given — and that argument bites hardest on the affirmative control itself: `Confirm_*_Btn` feeds `ConfirmBtn.Content`, so the red button that actually destroys the work read "Force Shutdown" in English next to a localized `İptal`. All **60** values are now translated — title, message *and* button, for all five operations — taking `es`/`fr`/`id`/`pl` as the house-style reference. Each locale's action verb matches its own `Palette_*Pc` command label (`Bilgisayarı Zorla Kapat` → `Zorla Kapat`, `Примусово вимкнути ПК` → `Примусове вимкнення`) so the dialog names the same operation as the button just pressed, and button labels take the same form as that file's existing `Confirm_DeleteRemote_Btn` (imperative in `hi`/`tr`, infinitive in `pt-BR`/`uk`). Verified mechanically across all 8 locales: every message retains its data-loss clause, no value names the opposite operation, and every Force variant carries a force/immediacy marker while no plain variant falsely claims one. Indonesian `Confirm_Restart_Btn` is deliberately left as "Restart" — that is the Indonesian loanword the rest of the `Confirm_*` block already uses (`Restart — Apakah Anda yakin?`, `Paksa Restart`, `di-restart`), not an untranslated string; translating it alone would make that dialog internally inconsistent. Key parity 1042; the neutral file is untouched. (4 resx files; RemEx-v5it.)
- **Half the PC FAQ was still English in five languages — and three answers were English in all eight.** The bead behind this described it as seven untranslated entries in Spanish; regenerating the audit at execution time found the defect was systematic rather than Spanish-only. Three classes were present: nine values byte-identical to English in `es`/`hi`/`pt-BR`/`tr`/`uk`; `Faq_Q3_Answer` and `Faq_Q5_Answer` half-translated, opening with raw English sentences before switching language mid-value; and `Faq_Q6/Q9/Q10_Answer` English in **all eight** locales *and* stale — they still described a generic VPN and an old file-transfer flow, predating the English text's Tailscale recommendation and its shared-folder/read-only-root explanation. So a non-English user reading the FAQ got English cards, sentences that changed language halfway, and outdated advice. 81 values across 8 files now translated from the current English, with each locale referencing the UI by the name it actually ships (`Transferencia de archivos`, `Palette de Commandes`, `Bilgisayarı Kilitle`, `Палітра команд`…) rather than the English label, sourced from the existing `Palette_*`/`FileTransfer_Header`/`CommandPalette_Title` keys. Commands (`ipconfig`, `ip addr`) and product names (Tailscale, WireGuard®, Wake-on-LAN, BIOS/UEFI) stay verbatim; OS UI labels follow each file's established habit — localized in `es`/`pl`/`pt-BR`/`tr`/`uk` as `fr` already did, left English in `hi`/`id`, which keep Windows labels in English throughout. Spanish stays uniformly informal per RemEx-9fn5, verified by a formal-marker sweep returning zero hits. Key parity 1042 across all 9 files; the neutral file is untouched. (8 resx files; RemEx-7jo9.)
- **Any file-manager request could hang the PC app indefinitely; now none of the control requests can.** Every request helper awaited its response with no bound at all, so a peer that never answers left the caller waiting forever with the spinner stuck on — and each one removed its pending-request entry only after a *successful* await, so every cancellation leaked one permanently. Eleven helpers fixed (roots, browse, delete, rename, manage, search, metadata, thumbnail, volumes, root add/remove), with timeouts chosen per operation class rather than shared: a single value would be wrong at both ends. **Review caught that the line had been drawn one operation-class too narrow, and the consequence was worse than a plain false failure:** copy and move stream the whole file before the peer replies, so their duration scales with file size — a multi-GB paste would have passed the deadline, reported "timed out", and then had the peer's eventual *success* reply discarded because the pending request was already cleaned up, telling the user the paste failed while the file appeared anyway. Those two now stay unbounded and move to the issue covering upload/download, where the right instrument is an idle watchdog rather than a deadline. Review also confirmed that copying a sibling class's habit of converting a timeout into a cancellation would have been a bug here, since one caller swallows cancellations silently. (`FileTransferClient.cs`; RemEx-q2bu.)
- **Verifying a file's hash no longer hangs the app forever.** Clicking Verify on a remote file waited indefinitely with the spinner stuck on: the PC sends a hash request, the Android client has no handler for that message type, and the wait had no bound at all — so the await never completed, the error path never ran, and the loading state never cleared. It also meant the message reworded for this failure one commit earlier was unreachable in practice. Now bounded using the pattern already in `ConnectionViewModel`, so it fails cleanly with a localized message and a logged timeout. A second defect in the same method went with it: the pending-request entry was only removed on the success path, so every cancellation leaked one. **The feature still does not *work* — it fails cleanly instead of hanging**, and making it work is filed as a real decision rather than a patch: either Android grows a handler, the type gets routed, and a capability is advertised so the button can be gated the way copy/move/mkdir/search already are, or the feature is removed. Neither host advertises a hash capability today, which is exactly why the button is offered unconditionally with nothing to gate on. **The defect is systemic:** all thirteen request helpers in that client share both faults, and the remaining twelve are filed with a full site inventory and an explicit warning not to use one timeout for all of them — search, thumbnails and the transfer-end waits are legitimately slow, and too aggressive a value would turn working operations into false failures. (`FileTransferClient.cs`; RemEx-0e54.)
- **The PC side of the file-transfer errors is fixed too, and it needed a logger first.** Five errors — browse, search, delete, rename and hash verify — were bare "X failed: {0}" passthroughs showing a raw .NET exception with no recovery action. 45 values across all 9 `.resx` files. The blocker was that `FileTransferViewModel` had no logger at all, so dropping the placeholder would have swallowed the exception entirely, turning developer-text-in-the-UI into a silent failure. The plumbing turned out smaller than expected because the pattern already existed: `ShellViewModel` already resolves a logger for a sibling hand-constructed view model, so this is an optional constructor parameter plus one `GetRequiredService` call — no DI registration, which would also have defeated the lazy construction that deliberately defers a network round-trip until the user opens Files. **Review caught a copy error worth naming:** the hash message said "Download it again", inferred from the key name `HashFailed`, but that method downloads nothing — it asks the phone for a SHA-256 of a file the user picked remotely. Read what the method does, not what its name implies. Two pre-existing bugs surfaced and were filed, one of them worse than the wording it was found beside: the Android client has **no handler** for the hash request and there is no client-side timeout, so Verify hangs indefinitely against a real phone — meaning the string this change just improved is currently unreachable. (`FileTransferViewModel.cs`, `ShellViewModel.cs`, all 9 `Strings*.resx`; RemEx-p9fn.)
- **File-transfer failures no longer put raw exception text on screen.** Eight errors interpolated a .NET exception or host protocol message straight into the UI — "Browse error: Access to the path is denied" — so the user got developer text and no way forward. 72 values rewritten across all 9 locales with the placeholders removed, and 11 call sites changed. The rule the change had to obey was *never drop a detail that has nowhere else to go*: two sites already logged their exception, the other nine gained a `Log.w` first, so the diagnostics are strictly better while the UI stops leaking jargon. **Review caught a regression we had introduced, and it is the instructive part:** two live paths already had *better* copy than the replacement — the host sends a fully-formed "this folder isn't one of the PC's shared folders, add it on the PC then try again", and a timeout returns an already-localized "Delete timed out". The generic message was factually wrong for both and discarded a correct remedy. Collapsing a family of messages is only safe when every member really shares the cause, so delete and rename now hedge across all three real causes, and the structural fix (distinguishing timeout from a host message, and letting user-grade host copy pass through) is filed — along with a pre-existing bug it exposed: the rename path returns the *delete* timeout string, while the rename one sits unused. Two keys were deliberately left alone after verification: one is orphaned only under a word-boundary check (a substring purge would delete its two live siblings), and one that *looks* like a file-transfer key is actually load-bearing in the remote-control screen. (`FileTransferViewModel.kt`, all 9 `strings.xml`; RemEx-ixsk.)
- **The most likely pairing failure of all — a wrong or expired PIN — no longer shows raw English developer text.** It was the one branch the previous change could not cover: `submitPin()` surfaced the native string verbatim, so "Pairing verification failed (incorrect PIN or session expired)" reached users in all 9 locales with no recovery action. A new `pairing_error_verify_failed` key wraps it in a localized sentence (parity 767 → 768, added to all nine files), covering all five reachable native causes with one next step that fits every one of them — they all clear the pairing session, so a fresh PIN really is required. **Review caught that the advice, as first written, dead-ended:** all five causes tear down the native session, but the Submit button stays enabled, so a user who got a fresh PIN and tapped Submit would hit "No active pairing session" and loop there indefinitely. The message now names Cancel first — the only working recovery — using each locale's own button wording, so the instruction matches the button actually on screen. The underlying UI problem (a live Submit on a dead session, with an existing `resetPairingState()` that has no callers) is filed separately. The three unreferenced pairing strings from the same issue were deliberately *not* deleted: two of them carry text already translated into all nine locales that a cause-code mapping would immediately need, so they are unwired rather than dead. (`PairingScreen.kt`, all 9 `strings.xml`; RemEx-nf79.)
- **Four different pairing failures no longer all say "Couldn''t reach your PC".** They were byte-identical, so four distinct causes were indistinguishable to the user and to anyone reading a support report; two more named a cause but offered no way out. All six now give a distinct cause and one next step, in all 9 locales, with the register taken from `pairing_error_timeout`, which already did this properly. **This was the third attempt** — the first died on unescaped apostrophes and two corrupting repair passes, the second on a design problem rewording alone could not fix: `reach_failed` and `unknown` were *nested inside* `start_failed` via `getString(start_failed, message)`, so giving each its own advice produced two next steps, two sentence terminators, a doubled Hindi danda, and contradictory instructions. Resolved by restructuring so no advice-bearing string is ever nested — matching the shape `submitPin()` already used — and reserving `start_failed` for the one branch whose content genuinely has no advice of its own. **A real bug fell out:** `start_failed` had no placeholder while the code passed it a detail argument, so the underlying reason was silently discarded on every occurrence; it now carries `%1$s`, and because the detail finally renders, its trailing period is trimmed so the user never sees "known**..**". Honest scope: five of the six are user-visible, but `pairing_error_cert_mismatch` has zero references, so its rewrite is a string-table improvement awaiting a display path — filed. (The *most common* pairing failure — wrong PIN or expired session — was still showing raw English at the time of that change; it is fixed in the entry below.) (`PairingScreen.kt`, all 9 `strings.xml`; RemEx-meqm.)
- **The personalization studio and all 26 card shape names now read in Turkish and Ukrainian.** 102 values across the `personalization_*`, `shape_*` and `font_*` families (the `tile_*` part of this slice landed in the previous change), grounded in each file's own existing wording — Turkish reuses "Kart Opaklığı", "Köşe", "Palet" and the "Dışavurumcu" its own font descriptor already used; Ukrainian reuses "Мова застосунку", "радіус кутів" and the screen titles established earlier. Three Ukrainian shape names genuinely require an apostrophe and use the backslash escape rather than being reworded around it, verified to decode correctly in the shipped APK rather than merely parse. **Review caught this bead's own mandated method propagating an error:** the instruction is to ground terms in the file's established vocabulary, and doing so made the Ukrainian opacity slider say "Прозорість" — *transparency* — because `faq_a9` already said that. Reading `PersonalizationScreen.kt` instead of the sibling string shows `1f` is fully opaque, so the slider read "Transparency: 100%" when cards were solid. Fixed in both places, with the wider inversion filed (Polish has it too). Grounding is only as reliable as what it grounds on, and for concepts whose wrong word is the *inverse* of the right one — opacity/transparency, enable/disable, allow/deny — the code that computes the value has to be read first. Turkish sentence-like English is now 60 and Ukrainian 49, from 213 and 204 when this work began. (`values-tr/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.1.)
- **The quick-settings tiles and widget picker now appear in Turkish and Ukrainian.** 71 values across the widget, dashboard and tile families. These matter more than their count suggests because several are visible *outside* the app: the eight `tile_*` keys are quick-settings tile labels declared in the manifest, and the widget label/description keys are what Android's own widget picker displays. Three uppercase strings needed care — Turkish uppercasing is locale-sensitive, since dotless `ı` becomes `I` but `i` becomes `İ` — so the uppercase form is authored directly in the resource and review confirmed no Kotlin `.uppercase()` sits in the call path to mangle it. The tile labels deliberately agree with the power-command strings shipped earlier, with Sleep differing in form (causative verb for the tile, noun for the menu) while staying unmistakable from Hibernate. Ukrainian also loses its only slang term: `widget_hardware_title` read "інформація про залізо" — literally "iron" — beside a new label saying "Обладнання", which would have shipped two words for hardware on one widget. **A reachability check produced a false verdict again, in a new way:** six widget keys reported as orphaned because the search covered only Kotlin, when they are referenced from `AndroidManifest.xml` and `res/xml/`. Android string references live in Kotlin (possibly line-wrapped), the manifest, widget metadata and layouts — a Kotlin-only search is not a reachability check. Turkish sentence-like English is now 85 and Ukrainian 79, from 213 and 204 when this work started. (`values-tr/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.4.)
- **Turkish and Ukrainian users no longer lose the localized button label at the moment they confirm a shutdown.** The previous change localized every power command, but `RemoteControlScreen` *replaces* the command's title with "Confirm choice" while confirmation is pending — and that string, the timer field beside it, and the Confirm button were all still English, so the one moment where comprehension matters most reverted to a language the user may not read. Now fully localized, and verified mechanically: all 24 string keys referenced anywhere on that screen are non-English in both locales. The change also swept the remaining English `button_*` keys (Connect, Done, Kill, Run, Select, Dismiss, Fetch from PC) and finished the task-manager kill dialog, whose title and body were still English while its button was being translated. Review corrected three things: the Ukrainian description said "additional delay" where the English says *optional*, losing the fact that the field can be left blank — chosen to dodge an apostrophe, on reasoning that didn't hold since the file already contains 29 working escapes; `button_fetch_from_host` had been fixed in Polish alone while French and Hindi still said "host", which makes the rest of that rename harder to audit (now all three, with the full class filed as its own sweep); and the scope was described as "the confirmation flow" when only 7 of the 13 keys are on that screen. (5 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-n602.)
- **The buttons that shut your PC down now say so in Turkish and Ukrainian.** 88 values across the remote-desktop, remote-control and remote-mouse screens. This slice needed more care than a label pass because `rc_shutdown`, `rc_force_restart`, `rc_hibernate` and friends really do the thing they name, and a mistranslation costs someone their unsaved work — so the category mapping was verified against `RemoteControlScreen.kt` rather than inferred from key names, confirming that the destructive group and the low-power group are labelled correctly and not swapped, and that sleep and hibernate are lexically unrelated in both languages. **A split slider label is fixed:** the FPS control read "Hedef FPS: 60" at 60 and "Target FPS: Sınırsız" at maximum, because the two strings that render on the same slider had only been half translated. The scroll-sensitivity labels turned out to be English in *seven* of eight locales, so they are finished in Spanish, French, Hindi, Indonesian and Brazilian Portuguese too; they carry a `%.1fx` specifier with no argument index, a class the write guard did not check, so the guard was extended before running. Review then caught that Turkish had three different "Kapat" strings on one screen with the most destructive one the least specific (now "Bilgisayarı Kapat"), and that Ukrainian's wake button sat one letter away from its shutdown button (now "Пробудити"). One review suggestion was deliberately declined: "Power" and "Energy" are near-synonyms, but that ambiguity comes from the English source, and fixing it in two locales is how nine files drift apart. (7 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.3.)
- **Six languages were telling users that shared folders "will arrive in a future update" — the feature already shipped.** `settings_ft_active` and its description still carried the pre-launch wording in Spanish, French, Hindi, Indonesian, Polish and Brazilian Portuguese, so those users were told a working feature did not exist yet. Verified before correcting it: `AndroidFileTransferHost.start()` is called unconditionally from the connection service, `FileHostHandler` is 900 lines with no stubs, and the settings row is behind no feature gate — asserting "it works" in six languages would have been worse than the stale text if that had been wrong. **This is the hardest class of localization rot to detect:** the six values were fluent, correct-looking prose describing behaviour the app no longer has, so no comparison against English can find them — they aren't English. What found them was reading the peer locales while pulling them up as translation models for something else. The same commit translates 33 file-transfer keys into Turkish and Ukrainian, grounded in the ~130 strings each file had already translated, restores the ↓/↑ glyphs Ukrainian had lost from its download/upload buttons, and corrects three locales that still told users to "connect to a host". Review caught a stray space before a comma that we had introduced in Polish, after which every non-French locale was swept for the same defect (zero remaining). (8 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.2.)
- **Screen readers now speak the icon labels in the user's own language.** The Android app already had content descriptions on its icons, but outside English every one of them was spoken in English — a blind Spanish or Hindi user heard "Camera", "Launch", "Category". 70 values across all 8 locales. **Measuring turned a 232-value job into a 70-value one:** of the 29 candidate keys, 13 have zero references in any Kotlin file, so translating them would have been 104 values spoken to nobody while making the accessibility numbers look better than they are (they are itemized on the resource-purge issue instead — prior work had already been spent translating four of them). Every label was checked against the icon at its call site, because a screen-reader label that names the wrong thing is worse than English: French takes `Appareil photo` for the QR scanner's camera rather than `Caméra`, which is a camcorder, and the play arrow that starts a stream takes each locale's media-"play" word because the button already renders its own "Start streaming" text and Compose merges the two. Values stay English where English *is* the word (Spanish "Error", Polish "Monitor"), while Hindi and Ukrainian transliterate because a Devanagari or Cyrillic voice handles a bare Latin token poorly. Review also corrected our reasoning on the keycap labels — the on-screen legends are hardcoded Latin literals, so the governing rule is WCAG 2.5.3 Label in Name rather than what a given keyboard prints — which reached the same answer on firmer ground and exposed three pre-existing conformance failures, now filed. (8 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-j33c.)
- **The persistent notification is no longer in English for everyone but Polish users.** Nine keys, 63 values across the seven remaining locales. This is among the most-seen text in the app — the notification sits in the system shade for the whole time RemEx is connected — and the channel name and description also appear in Android's own notification settings, outside the app entirely. Reachability was checked before translating this time, unlike the previous slice: all nine keys are genuinely referenced from Kotlin. Every concept was taken from a translation already in the same file (`status_connected` for "Connected", `cd_exit_fullscreen` for the exit verb) rather than invented, with Polish setting the register, and the real U+2026 ellipsis preserved rather than degraded to three dots. Turkish needed two rephrasings to stay apostrophe-free — "Bilgisayara bağlandı" instead of needing `PC'ye` — since a bare apostrophe makes aapt fail with a misleading unicode-escape error. Review verified in `RemexConnectionService.kt` that "Keeps the connection to your PC alive" is literally true before letting it ship in seven more languages, and caught a Brazilian-Portuguese value 32% longer than the English that would have truncated in the collapsed shade. (7 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-hnnx.)
- **The pairing dialog no longer appears in English to Spanish, French, Hindi, Indonesian, Polish and Brazilian-Portuguese users.** Eleven keys, 70 values — the four the issue named, plus the seven adjacent ones (the PIN field label and hint, the bad-PIN and certificate errors, the PIN prompt) that would otherwise have left the same dialog half-translated. **The audit that found this was much larger than the fix:** counting, for all 767 keys, how many of the 8 locales leave each byte-identical to English showed 51 keys English in *all eight* and 26 in seven of eight — a defect no audit had examined, because every previous pass scoped itself to Turkish and Ukrainian on the assumption the other six were a legitimate "technical floor". Roughly half of it is legitimate and is now recorded as such so future audits stop re-flagging it; the rest is split into issues, the sharpest being that ~25 icon content descriptions are English in every locale, so a blind user hears "Camera" and "Left Click" in any language. There is no single word for "pairing" to apply across six languages — `emparejamiento`, `couplage`, `पेयरिंग`, `penyandingan`, `parowanie`, `pareamento` — and two files disagree with themselves, so each term was counted in its own file before being chosen, and "Cancel" reuses each locale's existing translation rather than a fresh synonym. Review also corrected a claim of ours: four of these keys turn out to be unreferenced, and the pairing error users actually see arrives from `Remex.Core` as raw English developer diagnostics, now filed separately. (6 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-xzx4.)
- **Three strings that had gone stale in every language are correct again — and one of them was telling users something false.** These were not a translation gap but a staleness gap: the English was reworded and no locale followed. The FPS slider label claimed a "(max 120)" cap in all six other locales; the real ceiling is 360 (`DESKTOP_MAX_FPS`, and `DesktopConfig.MaxTargetFps` host-side agrees), and that label only renders at or below 240 anyway, so the cap was a leftover of a 120→360 bump that was simply untrue. The old-QR-code error still read "Please update your RemEx PC to 2.0." — in English — in es, fr, hi, in, pl and pt-BR, naming version 2.0 in an app shipping 2.4.0. And the language-picker note was missing the "applies immediately" clause in six locales while being wholly English in Turkish and Ukrainian; that claim was verified against `PersonalizationScreen.kt` before being asserted in eight languages, and it is true — `LocaleManager` on API 33+, `AppCompatDelegate` plus `recreate()` below it, no restart. **Why no audit had caught these:** coverage has always been measured as "values byte-identical to current English", and a stale value is not identical to current English, so it scores as translated while being both untranslated and out of date. The replacement detector is similarity-based rather than exact — an exact-match version found *zero*, because a past blanket Host→PC rename had run across the locale files too and rewritten the stale English in place so it matched no version of the English file. Tuning notes (a 40-character floor, since short values are dominated by correct cognates and loanwords) are recorded on the parity-CI issue. (all 8 `values-*/strings.xml`; RemEx-pve5.)
- **Turkish and Ukrainian now speak Turkish and Ukrainian on the connection, pairing and QR screens.** These two locales were four to five times further behind than the other six, and this is the first screen a new user sees, so a half-English pairing flow did the most damage here. Re-measuring changed the plan: tr had 213 sentence-like English values and uk 204, but 200 were the *same keys* — one backlog rendered twice, not two independent ones — so the work was split by screen with both locales in each slice, keeping them in step. Vocabulary is grounded in what these files already establish rather than a dictionary (`eşleştirme`, `Otomatik Keşfet`, `Uzak Masaüstü Varsayılanları`; `сполучення`, settled by `pairing_title` already shipping as "Сполучення"), and every value is written apostrophe-free so the aapt escaping hazard cannot apply. Three measurement blind spots were found and corrected along the way: filtering for "sentence-like" values hid 11 keys including `connection_label_subnet` = "Subnet Mask" at 11 characters; a line-oriented reachability grep wrongly reported six *live* keys as orphaned because the Kotlin source wraps the expression; and counting values byte-identical to English cannot see a value holding *stale* English, which is untranslated and out of date yet scores as translated. Review then caught the change being incoherent at its own edges — the `status_*` values that `connection_status_label` interpolates were English, so a Turkish user saw "Durum: Disconnected", and the instruction naming the Save & Connect button rendered that button in English — and, by dumping the compiled APK, caught bare double quotes being eaten by aapt2 at compile time. Sentence-like English is down 213→169 in tr and 204→163 in uk, with parity held at 767. (`values-tr/strings.xml`, `values-uk/strings.xml`; RemEx-c7hu.7.)
- **The last 11 unlabelled PC controls now have accessible names.** RemEx-dygk cut controls announcing a bare type name from 63 to 12 but deliberately left this class out of its icon-only scope; this finishes it. Ten toggles reuse the localized *title* key their own row already displays — no new resx keys — chosen over the adjacent description key because a screen reader wants the short label, and the on/off state is announced separately by the toggle pattern anyway. The two sensor checkboxes take per-item names bound to `SensorName`: one has a `TextBlock` as `Content` (precisely the case that announces a type name), the other has no content at all and is labelled by a sibling. Sweeping the whole project surfaced two more unlabelled checkboxes that were then left alone on purpose — both set `Content` to a localized string, which `ContentControlAutomationPeer` already resolves into the name. Every `ToggleSwitch` and `CheckBox` in `remex.desktop` is now either explicitly named or correctly named by its string content. (`SettingsView.axaml`, `PersonalizationPanelView.axaml`, `CanvasView.axaml`; RemEx-nu02.)
- **The PC app was close to unusable with a screen reader; it now names 90 controls instead of 1.** Exactly one `AutomationProperties.Name` existed across all 29 Avalonia views, and the consequence was worse than "icons are unlabelled". Avalonia's `ContentControlAutomationPeer` resolves a name from `AutomationProperties.Name`, then from the presenter child *only when `Content` is a string* — it never searches descendants. So every button whose content is a `Grid`, `StackPanel` or `Path` announced its **type name**: the entire nine-item navigation rail, both settings FABs, and all nine power commands — including Shutdown, Force shutdown and Restart to UEFI — read out as "Avalonia.Controls.Grid". 63 controls were in that state; 12 remain, and they are a different control class tracked as RemEx-nu02. Names are sourced from what each control already has — its localized tooltip, or its own visible label — so 5 new keys covered the entire pass: launcher tiles announce their app name, accent swatches their colour, and nothing invents a string. Controls whose `Content` is already a meaningful string were deliberately left alone, since a static name *overrides* a live one: the remote-desktop pad toggle keeps announcing "Pad: Compact"/"Pad: Full" rather than a fixed description. Reviewed by Opus (round-2 PASS; round 1 both corrected the descendant-search assumption above and caught one control where a static name masked a live localized one). (11 views, 9 resx files; RemEx-dygk.)
- **Non-English users were never told about the Android permission that is the usual reason discovery fails.** The PC FAQ's "auto-discovery isn't finding my PC" answer has five steps in English but only ever had four in every other language — the missing one being *"On Android 13+, allow Nearby Wi-Fi Devices; on Android 17+, allow Local Network access too."* That is the single most actionable line in the answer for a modern phone, and eight languages' worth of users were reading a troubleshooting list with the actual fix silently absent. Worse, in seven of those eight the answer was only half-translated: step 1 in the local language followed by three raw English lines. All 8 locales now carry the complete five-step answer, in step order matching the English, fully translated — and the question above it, still raw English in five locales, is translated too, since a translated answer under an English question reads worse than either. Each locale names the two Android permissions the way that locale's own Android Settings renders them (*Dispositivos cercanos*, *Appareils à proximité*, *Urządzenia w pobliżu*, *Yakındaki cihazlar*…) rather than transliterating the English, so the user can actually find the toggle. The Spanish Tailscale answer also drops its stray formal register — the Spanish FAQ is now uniformly informal. Key parity 1035; the neutral file was already correct and is untouched. (8 resx files; RemEx-9fn5.)
- **The last two PC FAQ answers that printed a visible `\n` at the user now render as real steps.** "Why can't my phone find my PC?" and the Tailscale setup walkthrough stored their line breaks as the literal two-character escape, and nothing in the path converts it — `AboutViewModel` hands the raw resource string to a plain `TextBlock`, so both answers appeared as one run-on paragraph with `\n` visible between every numbered step. They now use real line breaks, so the steps are steps. This clears the escape from the localization files entirely. Verified against a trap that caught the first scan: a case-insensitive search for the escape also matches the Windows account-path examples (`.\NomUtilisateur`, `.\NazwaUżytkownika`), which are legitimate and untouched — the check has to be case-sensitive. Key parity 1035; every value byte-identical to before apart from the intended conversion. (9 resx files; RemEx-mqwx.)
- **The PC FAQ no longer tells users to "verify your PC supports DXGI".** The remote-desktop-is-laggy answer listed that among its troubleshooting steps — unactionable for a non-technical user, and wrong twice over: DXGI Desktop Duplication is only the *middle* rung of an automatic WGC → DXGI → GDI ladder (PipeWire via the XDG portal on Linux), and nothing about it is the user's to verify. The answer now says plainly that RemEx picks the fastest capture method the PC supports, automatically, and otherwise lists only things the user can actually change: Quality, Target FPS, Scale, and the network — with the network called out as the usual culprit. The claim was checked against the code, not assumed: the capture-backend override is a registry-only operator knob (`HKLM`), with no setter anywhere in the PC UI, so "you don't need to set up screen capture" does not overclaim. All 8 locales previously held an *older English placeholder* rather than a translation and now have real ones, each naming the FPS control the way that locale's own UI labels it. The value also switches from a literal `\n` escape — which the FAQ's plain `TextBlock` binding rendered to the user as visible backslash-n — to real line breaks, so the bullets finally appear as bullets. `Faq_Q4_Answer` and `Faq_Q11_Answer` carried the same escape and were cleared separately under RemEx-mqwx. Key parity 1035. (9 resx files; RemEx-f8b5.)
- **The PC tutorial no longer tells users to configure apps "in the desktop client".** The App Launcher tutorial note read *"This works even with the standalone Remex.PC service — configure apps in the desktop client at least once."* Neither thing exists: there is one RemEx app on the PC that is both host and UI. Unlike the dead `Remex.PC.exe` strings removed in RemEx-o8n1, this one was live — bound at `ShellView.axaml:485` — so it reached every user who opened the tutorial, and it escaped that sweep only by saying "Remex.PC" without the `.exe`. It now states something true and more useful: "Your phone can only start programs you add here — for safety, RemEx won't launch anything that isn't on this list." Both claims were verified against the code rather than assumed — the Android client has no add/edit affordance at all (it only launches and picks apps for the widget), and `AppLauncherService` hard-fails with `UnauthorizedAccessException` unless the normalized path matches a persisted launcher entry, so the safety statement is literal, not marketing. Translated in all 9 languages, each matching the register its own locale already uses on that tutorial page (informal for Spanish, impersonal for Polish, formal elsewhere); the Hindi value also picks up the correct Devanagari danda in place of the CJK full stop it previously ended with. Key parity 1035. Remaining stale copy is routed, not forgotten: the still-live `Tutorial_P0_*` to RemEx-u4kw, and eight dead service-era keys to the RemEx-b5kx purge. Reviewed by Opus (round-2 PASS). (9 resx files; RemEx-phob.)
- **A failed remote-desktop frame no longer puts a .NET exception name in the PC status bar.** The status text read `Frame decode error (48213 bytes): InvalidDataException: ...` — a byte count and a CLR type name, shown to someone whose screen just went blank with no hint of what to do. It now reads "Can't display the remote screen — try lowering Quality, or disconnect and reconnect", naming the Quality control the user can actually see. The technical detail isn't lost: the `catch` previously swallowed the exception with no logging at all, and now emits a structured warning (frame size, consecutive-failure count, full exception and stack) to the in-memory log that feeds the exportable diagnostic report — so it lands in a bug report instead of in front of the user. Logged on the same "first 3 consecutive failures" cadence the status bar already used, so a sustained failure can't spam one entry per frame. The key was renamed `Status_FrameDecodeErrorFormat` → `Status_FrameDecodeError` since the new copy has no placeholders (key parity 1040 across all 9 files), and the Hindi, Brazilian Portuguese, Turkish, and Ukrainian variants — previously untranslated English placeholders — are now properly translated. Reviewed by Opus (round-2 PASS; round 1 caught that `RemoteDesktopViewModel` is hand-constructed, not DI-resolved, so the optional logger silently defaulted to `NullLogger` and logged nothing — it is now passed explicitly from `ShellViewModel`). (`RemoteDesktopViewModel.cs`, `ShellViewModel.cs`, 9 resx files; RemEx-sbck.)
